Demon: Strong’s Exhaustive Concordance #1140 daimonion; a demonic being; by extension a deity: devil, god.

King James Version translates this word as “devil” 59 times, “god” 1 time

Thayer’s Greek-English Lexicon: 1) the divine power, deity, divinity 2) a spirit, a being inferior to God, superior to men 3) evil spirits or the messengers and ministers of the devil

All uses of this word in the King James Version after the Gospels and excluding the one use in Revelation:

Acts 17:18: Then certain philosophers of the Epicureans, and of the Stoicks, encountered him. And some said, What will this babbler say? other some, He seemeth to be a setter forth of strange gods <1140>: because he preached unto them Jesus, and the resurrection.

1 Corinthians 10:20: But I say, that the things which the Gentiles sacrifice, they sacrifice to devils <1140>, and not to God: and I would not that ye should have fellowship with devils <1140>.

1 Corinthians 10:21: Ye cannot drink the cup of the Lord, and the cup of devils <1140>: ye cannot be partakers of the Lord’s table, and of the table of devils <1140>.

1 Timothy 4:1: Now the spirit speaketh expressly, that in the latter times some shall depart from the faith, giving heed to seducing spirits, and doctrines of devils <1140>;

James 2:19: Thou believest that there is one God; thou doest well: the devils <1140> also believe, and tremble.

Another interesting account that shows Satan’s influence without one being possessed is Elymas the magician. Note he is called a son of the devil, but Paul does not treat him as one who is possessed:

Acts 13:4–12: (NASB) 4 So, being sent out by the holy spirit, they went down to Seleucia and from there they sailed to Cyprus. 5 When they reached Salamis, they began to proclaim the word of God in the synagogues of the Jews; and they also had John as their helper. 6 When they had gone through the whole island as far as Paphos, they found a magician, a Jewish false prophet whose name was Bar-Jesus, 7 who was with the proconsul, Sergius Paulus, a man of intelligence. This man summoned Barnabas and Saul and sought to hear the word of God. 8 But Elymas the magician (for so his name is translated) was opposing them, seeking to turn the proconsul away from the faith. 9 But Saul, who was also known as Paul, filled with the holy spirit, fixed his gaze on him, 10 and said, You who are full of all deceit and fraud, you son of the devil, you enemy of all righteousness, will you not cease to make crooked the straight ways of the Lord? 11 Now, behold, the hand of the Lord is upon you, and you will be blind and not see the sun for a time. And immediately a mist and a darkness fell upon him, and he went about seeking those who would lead him by the hand. 12 Then the proconsul believed when he saw what had happened, being amazed at the teaching of the Lord.

### Study Questions

https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/1357-exorcisms/

1. What is exorcism? What does it mean to “exorcize” (verb) or “adjure”? How was “adjure” used legally in

the Old Testament? Why would the Jewish exorcists adjure by Jesus’ name? (Acts 19:11-13, Matthew 26:63,

Leviticus 5:1)

2. During Jesus’ earthly ministry, who was given the power to cast out demons? What kind of special rituals

did Jesus and the disciples use to cast out demons? After the two instances of being sent out, in what way were the followers of Jesus involved in healing before Pentecost? (Matthew 10:5-8, Mark 6:12-13,

Luke 10:1-3, 17-20)

3. In view of 1 Peter 5:8, why do true Christians (and ancient Israelites) not have to worry about demon

possession? Are we totally protected against Satan? Explain how 1 Corinthians 10:16-21 shows us this. Which influences of Satan’s give you the most trouble?

4. Recount the story of the man with the unclean spirit in the country of the Gergesenes (Gadarenes). What

were the demons afraid Jesus would do to them? Compare their lives to Jesus’. What were the possible reasons the demons begged to enter the swine? Was there a lesson for the Jewish pig farmers in the region? Why does Jesus release the spirits instead of destroying them? (Mark 5:1-13)

5. What method did Jesus use to cast out demons? How did he handle the demon in the daughter of the

Canaanite woman? Why would he even consider healing a Gentile girl? (Matthew 15:22,28)

6. Describe all the accounts of Jesus’ followers casting out demons after Pentecost. What happened at

Pentecost that would have helped them to do this? What rituals were used in all these examples? What other gift of the spirit was casting out demons connected to? Who were healed by the disciples? Contrast healing practices in Christian churches now with the apostles’ time. (Acts 5:12-16, 8:5-8, 16:16-19, 19:11-12)

7. Do you feel it is a Christian responsibility to cast out demons at this time? Why do you think there are so

few instances of demon possession in the New Testament?

8. What do the Scriptures tell us to be like in this day and age? Think on these points:

a. How do you actively pursue and show evidence of your faith every day?

b. Are your goals and moral stands based on the world’s or on Christ’s?

c. How does God’s spirit in your life guide and direct your actions?

d. How do you attempt to honor God in all that you do?

## Episode 1358 — What’s the Difference Between Burnt Offerings and Sin Offerings?
Published: 2024-11-04
Episode page: https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/1358-sin-offering/
Rewind PDF: https://resources.christianquestions.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/11/08150120/CQ_Rewind_11-4-24_Whats_the_Difference_Between_Burnt_Offerings_and_Sin_Offerings.pdf

What’s the Difference Between Burnt Offerings and Sin Offerings?

Hebrews 13:11: For the bodies of those animals whose blood is brought into the holy place by the high priest as an offering for sin, are burned outside the camp.

Special Guest: Tom Ruggirello

The Old Testament Law given to Moses after Israel’s deliverance from Egypt was filled with rituals and sacrifices. This was all designed to teach God’s people three basic principles. These principles are fundamental for any civilization that seeks to perpetuate itself. The first principle is to comply with righteous and just rules that transcend human desires. For Israel, this meant they could have God's favor, protection and leadings if they remained in harmony with His commands. This required sacrifices. The second principle is to live with humble gratitude for the freedoms that righteousness and justice provide. Israel was to learn this power of thankfulness by practicing heart gratitude towards God for all of His mercies. The third principle is devotion—heartfelt devotion to righteousness as a result of humble gratitude. All of this required animal sacrifices. Why?

Our guest, Tom Ruggirello, is a good friend and, more importantly, a Christian brother. He has been with us on several previous episodes.

Tom has been a minister with the Chicago Bible Students Congregation for many years. He gives sermons regularly and leads Bible studies. Tom is also dedicated to working in the Christian print shop where Bible study materials are printed.

Tom is “all in” on preaching the gospel and has been an ardent supporter of the work done at Christian Questions for many years.

Tom’s comments will be paraphrased in the color brown throughout this Rewind.

We’re presenting this topic because this question is one of the most popular questions asked by the public on internet search engines which leads them to our website, ChristianQuestions.com.

Once Israel was established as a nation, God meticulously instructed them through Moses to build the Tabernacle, which would be their portable place of worship as they wandered in the wilderness. Tom, to start, remind us why we, as Christians, should study the structure of the Tabernacle and the meaning of the animal sacrifices God required of the Jewish people. Shouldn’t we be focusing only on Jesus? Can you establish the astounding importance of the Tabernacle to us?

If I had to summarize the Tabernacle in one word, I would use the word, “sacrifice.” The Tabernacle symbolically focuses on Jesus’ sacrifice as well as that of his followers. Jesus told his disciples, take up the cross and follow me (Mark 10:21). That’s not the Prosperity Gospel we hear today. Following Christ means giving up our wills and working in his service. It’s not about worldly prosperity.

The Tabernacle must be important for Christians to consider since the Apostle Paul deals with it fairly extensively in the book of Hebrews. He writes about the symbolism God put there for us to study. Symbolism is interesting—it is something God uses to help us understand what He is looking for in our lives today. So, I find it very helpful in trying to relate the various sacrifices not only to the life of Christ, but to my life as well.

God uses the Tabernacle to illustrate certain important principles that are necessary for us to eventually gain eternal life. As we study the Tabernacle, we will see how important they are.

In the opening comments, we mentioned three important principles:

1. JUSTICE

2. GRATITUDE

3. HEART DEVOTION

These principles are being taught through the ancient Hebrew Tabernacle and its rituals.

1. JUSTICE: Justice is a key principle that is a fundamental part of the Tabernacle system. It makes the point that because God’s justice condemned our race, we cannot have the same relationship with Him Adam once had. Therefore, we learn from the Tabernacle that God’s justice must be satisfied before we can have a relationship with Him. That is clearly shown in the sin offering on Israel’s annual Day of Atonement, which we will discuss later.

2. GRATITUDE: Another powerful lesson inherent to the Tabernacle is that God is looking for sincere heart appreciation. The satisfaction of justice is important—it is vital! Knowing how it is accomplished should move us to the next step—heartfelt gratitude to God. That’s our part where we begin to see what God is looking for in us.

3. HEART DEVOTION: This third principle builds on our understanding of justice and our sincere gratitude for its satisfaction. This personal focus leads us to the result of heart devotion. The third type of offering shows a deep devotion, or consecration, to God. God is looking for us to eventually consecrate our lives to Him.

These three principles are fundamental to everything that took place in the Tabernacle. Once we put them in order, we will see the depth and importance of this study.

We will discuss many interesting details, so if you have questions or need greater detail on this topic, email us at inspiration@ChristianQuestions.com.

We will discussing sin offerings, burnt offerings and peace offerings which were sacrifices ordered by God to be performed within the Tabernacle.

Every burnt offering in Chapter 1 of Leviticus was described in this way:

Leviticus 1:9: (KJV) …and the priest shall burn all on the altar, to be a burnt sacrifice, an offering made by fire, of a sweet savour unto the LORD.

The word savor makes us think of food that is very satisfying. Using this analogy is saying our heart devotion is so satisfying that He symbolically tastes it as something sweet. In the Tabernacle, God illustrates practical principles we can use in our Christian lives. This makes it fascinating! If we know something pleases Him, we can focus on it.

First, we learn that we need atonement, and beyond that God is looking for our devotion to Him.

This is not a technical definition, but we sometimes break down the word atonement to be “at-one-ment.”

In this case, the parties are God and mankind. They are currently opposed because sin entered and broke the tie that once existed.

Before we get too far into the lesson, we have to ask why sacrifice animals? Why not fruit, vegetables, or something people could make?

That is a good question. Certain principles could only be conveyed with the sacrifice of a life. The Scriptures tell us that when Adam sinned, the only way to redeem him was to sacrifice a price equal to Adam, a perfect life for a perfect life. That could not be illustrated with vegetables or fruits. It had to be with a life. Since it was not time for Jesus to offer his life, an animal was used to symbolize what was to come in Jesus. Life is precious and the redemption of life has a high price.

There is a huge picture here that is not just about the moment of the sacrifices in ancient Israel but is actually about eternity. This is a powerful reminder of what sacrifice really means when we look at the sacrifice of Jesus, which will come up repeatedly as we go through this Tabernacle study together.

Now let’s look at the various categories of sacrifices depicted in the Tabernacle:

There’s a lot of fine detail in the Tabernacle, but when we step back and look at the big picture, it’s not that complicated.

Sin offerings were required by God in order to maintain Israel’s relationship with Him.

Let’s read where the first animal of Israel’s annual sin offering is described:

Leviticus 16:11: Then Aaron shall offer the bull of the sin offering which is for himself and make atonement for himself and for his household, and he shall slaughter the bull of the sin offering which is for himself.

Although God required sin offerings, the other two categories were not required but voluntary. Any Israelite was free to bring a sacrifice that expressed their gratitude and devotion to God.

We read about people’s burnt offerings in Leviticus Chapter 1:

Leviticus 1:2-3: 2 Speak to the sons of Israel and say to them, When any man of you brings an offering to the LORD, you shall bring your offering of animals from the herd or the flock. 3 If his offering is a burnt offering from the herd, he shall offer it, a male without defect; he shall offer it at the doorway of the tent of meeting (the “tent of meeting” is another term for the Tabernacle), that he may be accepted before the LORD.

In addition to burnt offerings, a third category was peace offerings. These were always connected with burnt offerings because a peace offering was to be placed on top of a burnt offering. So, these two offerings were directly related.

Peace offerings are described in Leviticus 3:

Leviticus 3:1: Now if his offering is a sacrifice of peace offerings, if he is going to offer out of the herd, whether male or female, he shall offer it without defect before the LORD.

We have several kinds of offerings here; obviously, there is a difference between burnt offerings and sin offerings. We also have a peace offering on top (literally) of all this.

Is there a specific sequence in these three offerings?

Yes, the sequence is significant. Only after Israel’s sins were first atoned for by the annual sin offerings were the Israelites free to express their personal gratitude by bringing a burnt offering. Then, their personal peace with God was established when they brought a peace offering. So, the logical sequence is to first accept the atonement sacrifices followed by their willing expressions of gratitude and devotion.

This overview is the central theme of the Tabernacle:

- First, national atonement
- Followed by personal gratitude
- Resulting in individual peace with God

We can stop there and get the general sense of the Tabernacle picture, but there’s beauty in the details of each of these categories.

Tom has given us the order: three different kinds of offerings, each in a logical sequence, and each telling a different part of the story. This is just beginning to unfold.

As is typical with the Bible and God and His plan, these practices teach much broader lessons when we put things in order.

One detail that immediately jumps out here is the meticulous ordering of events that God required for Israel to attain and maintain His favor.

So far, we see the big picture that shows us different sacrifices for different purposes. What do the details of each kind of sacrifice reveal?

Because these sacrifices have an implied sequence, we need to start at the beginning, which would be with the justice required to have God’s favor, as nothing else matters without His favor. Our starting and main point will be the Day of Atonement sacrifices, known as “sin offering” sacrifices. We will start with these and build a foundation.

What was the Day of Atonement, when was it and why were these sacrifices SO important?

Leviticus 16:30-31: 30 For it is on this day that atonement shall be made for you to cleanse you; you will be clean from all your sins before the LORD. 31 It is to be a sabbath of solemn rest for you, that you may humble your souls; it is a permanent statute.

The Day of Atonement was observed once a year. We know it today as “Yom Kippur.” Yom means “day,” and Kippur means “to cover.” So, it’s the day of covering Israel’s sins.

For Israel, the covering of their sins was accomplished by the yearly sacrifice of three animals: a bull and two goats. Today, we will focus on the first two, the young bull and a goat, referred to as the Lord’s goat.

- The bull was offered to cover the sins of the high priest and the other priests.
- The Lord’s goat was for the sins of the people of Israel.

Leviticus 16:6: Then Aaron shall offer the bull for the sin offering which is for himself, that he may make atonement for himself and for his household.

Leviticus 16:15: Then he shall slaughter the goat of the sin offering which is for the people, and bring its blood inside the veil and do with its blood as he did with the blood of the bull, and sprinkle it on the mercy seat and in front of the mercy seat.

The mercy seat was the lid of the Ark of the Covenant, which was the only piece of furniture in the Most Holy compartment of the Tabernacle.

This seems odd. Why were there different sin offerings? Wasn’t one enough?

It would seem that offering the bull would be enough to atone for the sins of all Israel. So, clearly there is an additional message by adding the Lord’s goat as part of the sin offering. He is saying that the class pictured by the goat has something to offer in cleansing the world of sin. We will get into what when we see what the Apostle Paul says in Hebrews 13.

To get a sense of what Leviticus 16 is describing, we have to first envision what the Tabernacle looked like.

Tom last spoke to us on our companion Episode #1099: “Should Christians Care About the Jewish Tabernacle?”

The Tabernacle was a portable place of worship constructed of a large rectangular courtyard with a smaller tent structure within it.

When you first entered the courtyard, you saw a copper altar for animal sacrifices.

As you went a little further towards the tent, there was a piece called the laver. It was where the priest would wash before entering the tent before him.

That tent was divided into two rooms. The first and larger room was called the Holy. In the Holy were three items:

1. A wooden table overlaid with gold, containing 12 loaves of unleavened bread. It was called the table of shewbread.

2. Across from this table was a golden candlestick with seven branches, each branch with a cup filled with olive oil. It was the source of light for the Holy.

3. Then, before the second curtain that went into the next room was an incense altar, again made of wood, overlaid with gold, where incense was burned.

4. Now, as we are standing in the Holy, we walk forward from there to the next smaller room which was called the Most Holy. This was the most important room. It only contained the Ark of the Covenant which represented God’s presence with Israel.

The top of the Ark of the Covenant was called the Mercy Seat. There was a bright light above the Mercy Seat in the Most Holy often called the Shekinah glory. “Shekinah” comes from the Hebrew word “shakan,” meaning to reside or dwell. So “Shekinah glory” means God’s presence was dwelling with Israel.

In Exodus 25:22, God says to Moses, There I will meet with you from above the mercy seat, from between the two cherubs that are upon the ark of the covenant.

Back to the Day of Atonement: after putting on sacrificial robes, the high priest killed the bull in the courtyard of the Tabernacle. Standing beside the altar he caught some of its blood in a special container.

The high priest then took the blood into the Most Holy and sprinkled it seven times. It says he sprinkled the blood upon the mercy seat eastward; and before the mercy seat (Leviticus 16:14). That description may suggest the blood was sprinkled in the shape of a cross and points to the intended symbolism, that the sacrifice of the bull represented the sacrifice of Jesus who died on a cross.

That little detail gives us a sense of God’s foresight in His plan—He imprinted or put a picture of Jesus' sacrifice into that ancient time thousands of years ago. It wasn’t just in the bull and the blood but in how the sacrifice was offered. This remarkable evidence shows the importance of studying these Old Testament pictures because they will help us understand the New Testament in ways we cannot imagine.

To give a foundation to our Christian connection, here is the Apostle Paul’s observation:

Hebrews 9:11-12: 11 But when Christ appeared as a high priest of the good things to come, he entered through the greater and more perfect tabernacle, not made with hands, that is to say, not of this creation; 12 and not through the blood of goats and calves, but through his own blood, he entered the holy place once for all, having obtained eternal redemption.

These two enlightening verses tell us that Jesus fills a number of roles pictured on the Day of Atonement:

First, Jesus is pictured by the high priest who offered the sacrifices.

Then, his sacrificed life is pictured by the blood that was taken into the Most Holy.

Jesus is pictured in various ways in this Day of Atonement arrangement. Why? Wasn't his sacrificed life, pictured by the bull, enough? He came to give his life for the world. What more is needed? Yet, he is showing up everywhere in the picture.

Well, in a way, he does! It shows how comprehensive the role of Jesus is in God’s plan. The work of Jesus is far more than just dying, which, of course, is crucial. It goes beyond his death.

He is also the great high priest who oversees the second aspect of atonement, the actual cleansing of sin. As the great high priest, he will rule the millennial kingdom, where that work will be undertaken. But we will get into that in more detail. The role of Jesus encompasses so much more than just taking away the sin; the atonement this symbolism represents in the Tabernacle heightens and highlights that work.

We know the blood of goats and calves cannot permanently take away sin, but Jesus’ blood was the actual cleansing of sin, a sacrifice once for all time. The Tabernacle pictured this for us repeatedly each year on the Day of Atonement. We see a dramatic connection between the Old and New Testaments.

It is inspiring, even breathtaking, to realize that God, in His foresight, showed us all that Jesus would accomplish centuries before he came!

We talked about the bull as the sin offering, but what about the goat, which is also mentioned in Leviticus 16?

This goat obviously plays an important role in the Atonement Day rituals and carries a different meaning. Interestingly, it was treated in the same manner as the bull. The high priest killed it beside the altar, and its blood was also sprinkled on and before the mercy seat. What, then, does this goat represent?

To keep all of this straight, we are offering a free PDF chart of the various sacrifices. We will mail a hard copy to our listeners in the United States. Just write us at inspiration@ChristianQuestions.com.

The Apostle Paul addresses this in Hebrews 13, and it adds another dimension to the meaning of Israel’s Day of Atonement:

Hebrews 13:11-13: 11 For the bodies of those animals whose blood is brought into the holy place by the high priest as an offering for sin, are burned outside the camp. 12 Therefore Jesus also, that he might sanctify the people through his own blood, suffered outside the gate. 13 So, let us go out to him outside the camp, bearing his reproach.

One detail we need to know is not only what was done with the blood but also what was done with the rest of the sacrificed animal. We mentioned that the blood was first sprinkled on the Mercy Seat in the Most Holy. Then, out in the court, the kidneys and liver, along with the fat, were the only parts of the bull and goat that were burned on the altar (Leviticus 4:9). These were the animal's organs used to clean out their body, so offering them showed that the purpose of these offerings was to cleanse the nation of sins.

Most of us would read about the kidneys and livers and think, it’s kind of gross; I don’t need to understand this. Why would we specify those things? Because every detail was important. God laid it out this way to show us things we need to understand in picture form so that when we see Jesus later, it unfolds the reality of what he gave for the world of mankind.

We now know what was done with the blood and two organs. In Hebrews 13, Paul is referring to the fact that the bodies of the animals were taken outside the camp of Israel and burned. He says that the first carcass, the young bull, represents the suffering of Jesus.

Verse 13 adds a remarkable detail. He says, let us go unto him outside the camp, bearing his reproach. He describes the symbolism of how the goat was treated exactly like the bull. It was slain in the court. Its blood was sprinkled on the Mercy Seat, its liver and kidneys were burned on the altar and its body was taken outside the camp of Israel and burned.

He relates that to “us.” The us refers to the brethren Paul was addressing. They are pictured in the goat, the second part of the sin offering. It then extends to all the followers of Christ. Faithful Christians are part of the “goat class” that will be involved in the work of atonement.

Hold on now! As Christians, we always look at Jesus as the offering for sin. But, Tom, are you saying here that the Lord’s goat is actually picturing the followers of Jesus as well?

Yes, that’s right. We learn from this that covering the sins of the world is only part of the work of atonement. For example, when you put a blanket over something, you’re not actually changing anything. You’re just hiding what’s underneath. However, the work of atonement is not only a covering of sin, but the eventual purpose is to remove the effects of sin in every human heart. It’s taking the sin out of the sinner and making that person actually pure and perfect before God.

The physical offering for sin back with the ancient Israelites represented a covering to hide their sins so they could have a relationship with God, but underneath that covering, they were still the same sinful people. But, in the kingdom, Jesus and his most faithful followers, his church, will help people ACTUALLY live without sin—not with just this covering.

It is profound that God set this up thousands of years ago to show us the work of Jesus and his church in the future!

Let’s put that in the context of God’s kingdom. Jesus died as a ransom price for Adam. By doing so, he purchased not only Adam but also his descendants, the entire human race, which guarantees their resurrection in the kingdom.

But when they come back from death, they will be the same individuals who died with the same sinful tendencies. And so, the second aspect of atonement will be the work of removing sin in their hearts and minds.

This cleansing aspect of atonement will be the work of the millennium, after the resurrection, and the Scriptures tell us the church will share in that work:

1 Corinthians 15:21-22: 21 For since by a man came death, by a man also came the resurrection of the dead. 22 For as in Adam all die, so also in Christ all will be made alive.

Being made alive means more than being resurrected. It means being brought to human perfection and granted eternal life.

Revelation 20 describes the 1,000-year reign of Christ and the church, calling them priests of God. The work of this priesthood will be so much more significant than Israel’s priesthood was. It will bring every willing individual back into harmony with God. In the kingdom, people won't need to have their sins just covered—those sins will be removed.

Bringing them to human perfection will mean each individual will have their own personal relationship with God, just as Adam once had. In fact, it is even better because they will have the experience of sin to draw from and remember what it was like without God.

The covering of sins in the Tabernacle arrangement through the sacrifices of the Day of Atonement—with the bull representing Jesus and the Lord’s goat representing the faithful followers of Christ—had to be done again and again. They had to be repeated each year because these sacrifices of bulls and goats could not permanently remove the sins of the people. But because Jesus came to earth and died as a ransom price for Adam's sin, his sacrifice ensured the covering of sins for all in the resurrection. No other sacrifice will ever be needed because he was the one to give his life once for all. This is the highest level of justice and righteousness. It is utterly praiseworthy to look at this and marvel at what the Old Testament picture shows us, not only through Jesus’ sacrifice 2,000 years ago but also at what Jesus means for eternity!

If we're faithful in our life as Christians, the role God has planned for us is to be part of the priesthood that will help remove sin from the hearts and minds of every willing individual. There’s a practical reason why Christians are called to be part of this priesthood and to share in that work.

Paul writes to fellow believers:

1 Corinthians 10:13: (Williams New Testament) No temptation has taken hold of you but what is common to human nature…

What can we as individuals possibly offer to help bring the world back to a relationship with God?

When we look at this subject, we might first think we can’t offer anything. Faithful Christians are tempted just like everyone else. But God knows that it gives them something of great value—the personal experience of being sinners themselves. That real- life experience will be important as mankind comes back from the grave. They will know that the ones ministering from heaven are a group of individuals who, along with Christ, understand the struggles they have experienced in this life. It will help create a sympathetic bond between mankind and those in heaven who are guiding and helping them overcome the tendencies of sin.

Even though we have nothing to offer of value in terms of the justice of a perfect life for a perfect life—Jesus’ death took care of that—we have something to offer in terms of experience. We have undergone sinful experiences, working through and overcoming our weaknesses so we can be sympathetic to the rest of the world. It is a beautiful picture of how Jesus pays the price and how we get to work with him in the ministry of reconciliation of mankind to God.

Besides the need to offer his life as a ransom, Hebrews 2 tells us that even Jesus needed to learn obedience:

Hebrews 2:17-18: 17 Therefore, he had to be made like his brethren in all things, so that he might become a merciful and faithful high priest in things pertaining to God, to make propitiation (satisfaction) for the sins of the people. 18 For since he himself was tempted in that which he has suffered, he is able to come to the aid of those who are tempted.

We know Jesus had to come to this earth so he could die. But Paul tells us that another reason Jesus came to this world was to become a merciful and faithful high priest. Although he was not a sinner, he knows what sin is. He saw the effects firsthand. He felt the suffering it brought.

There’s the second work of atonement. In the kingdom, no one will be able to say: you can’t understand why I am the way I am because the ones administering the work of the kingdom will be people who were there themselves. Jesus and the church lived on this earth and experienced life like everyone else. That gives meaning to a Christian’s life because it tells us what we are being prepared for.

This helps us understand the dedication part of this equation. When we realize that the trial of a true Christian who has been given God’s spirit in this period of time is to prepare them to be part of the priesthood along with Jesus, we realize it is an amazing privilege. It is almost beyond comprehension. Gratitude is awesome, but for that gratitude to transform into committed action is another thing altogether.

There is more to the sin offering sacrifices, right? Leviticus also details special sacrifices of a ram, a lamb, other goats, and even a pigeon. Are there multiple bullocks or only one?

Yes, we’ve only touched on the Atonement Day sin offerings and how they represent offerings that the world cannot provide for itself. However, there were other sin offerings that individual Israelites were expected to bring.

For example, in Leviticus 6, if someone stole something or cheated someone, they were expected to bring a ram for a sin offering and not only make full restitution of what they stole but pay an additional 20 percent. This shows that in the kingdom, people will be held responsible for their ongoing sins.

So, the sin offerings were all-encompassing, from the Day of Atonement sacrifices that will provide the opportunity for the world to be restored to holding each individual responsible for their ongoing trespasses. It is a development process the world will go through.

We just discussed the example in Leviticus 6 of someone stealing or cheating and having to bring an offering and pay an additional penalty. In the Old Testament Law and the sacrifices of the Tabernacle, we see every single principle of righteousness that God will ultimately require from all mankind once the kingdom is established.

It is remarkable to see the depth of meaning in the Tabernacle, in those gross and bloody sacrifices that teach us so much. They give us a deeper appreciation than we would have had if we had just stayed in the New Testament scriptures. We have to study the whole Bible.

We offer up our lives to Him as a meaningful expression of our gratitude through the devotion of attempting to follow in Jesus' footsteps daily. This is remarkable! Seeing these connections and this powerful meaning can only be accomplished by combining the Old and New Testaments!

We have thus far focused on the solemn importance of the sin offering sacrifices. What about the burnt offering and peace offering sacrifices?

Having firmly established the justice part of the equation, we can now look at the part of gratitude. Remember, no one can truly come into God’s favor without having that pathway appropriately opened through the satisfaction of justice. Now, the question is, how do we show our humble gratitude for being able to be on this path?

We have a feeling it has something to do with these offerings! Specifically, how do these burnt offerings and peace offerings differ from the Atonement Day sin offerings?

Interestingly, the first category God gave Israel in Leviticus 1 was burnt offerings, not sin offerings, as we might expect. Along with peace offerings, burnt offerings are described as a sweet savor unto the Lord. Sin offerings are never described this way.

But calling burnt and peace offerings a sweet savor tells us that God takes particular pleasure in these two types of offerings, which may be why they are listed before sin offerings. Not describing sin offerings as a sweet savor suggests that God takes no pleasure in the required suffering and death of either animals in Israel, or by our Lord and his church. They are simply a requirement of justice.

This appears to be the reason these offerings brought Him great joy:

Leviticus 1:3: (KJV) If his offering be a burnt sacrifice of the herd, let him offer a male without blemish: he shall offer it of his own voluntary will at the door of the tabernacle of the congregation before the LORD.

Just as sin offerings were never a sweet savor to God, neither were they described as freewill offerings. When we discussed how Jesus was a sin offering, we saw it from a legal standpoint; it was a requirement of God’s justice. But how Jesus gave his sacrifice—this is what made it a sweet savor to God.

Ephesians 5:1-2: 1 Therefore be imitators of God, as beloved children; 2 and walk in love, just as Christ also loved you and gave himself up for us, an offering and a sacrifice to God as a fragrant aroma.

We have spent all our time to this point focusing on the sin offering—the justice, the requirements, and how it had to be perfect. But the burnt offering was much more of a gratitude offering. It was something given from the heart rather than to appease justice.

Does this reference to the offering of Jesus as a fragrant aroma show us Jesus’ own personal dedication and willingness? Does this go beyond his offering for sin?

Yes, to both questions! Jesus’ sacrifice was a sweet aroma—a sweet savor to God. It shows us his attitude when making his offering. He offered himself willingly. That willingness to serve others and to do God’s will with a glad heart gives God pleasure. It should be a reminder to us of the heart attitude God is looking for.

His attitude is seen prophetically. Applying this to Jesus is perfect:

Psalms 40:8-10: (KJV) 8 I delight to do Thy will, O my God: yea, Thy law is within my heart. 9 I have preached righteousness in the great congregation: lo, I have not refrained my lips, O LORD, thou knowest. 10 I have not hid Thy righteousness within my heart; I have declared Thy faithfulness and Thy salvation: I have not concealed Thy lovingkindness and Thy truth from the great congregation.

That is the sweet savor of Christ. He loved what he was doing, and speaking of that and His heavenly Father came from his heart.

This rounds out the depth of Jesus’ sacrifice. The sin offering shows us the legal part of what Jesus did. He had to fulfill justice—a perfect man (Jesus) for a perfect man (Adam). The Day of Atonement illustrates that legal sacrifice was necessary to take away sin, year after year. But when Jesus came, he gave himself as a sacrifice once—and it was done.

I delight to do Thy will, O my God, shows the heart behind that legal approach. It shows his desire to please his Father. Thy law is within my heart! That is what we see in that sweet aroma. There is such a difference between the legal sin offering and the joyous burnt offering with its sweet savor. When we look at Jesus’ sacrifice from the pictures and symbolism of the Tabernacle, we look at the New Testament, and it’s just like it bursts open with this brand new life!

What about peace offerings? How are they different from sin offerings and burnt offerings, and what can we learn from them?

In addition to burnt offerings, peace offerings were voluntary and considered a sweet savor to God. One interesting aspect of peace offerings was that they were to be laid on top of the daily burnt offering, called the continual burnt offering (Exodus 29:38,39).

The daily burnt offering consisted of two lambs. They were the first and last sacrifices of every day. One was offered in the morning at 9 am and the other in the evening at 3 pm. (For Israel, the evening began around 3 pm.)

This burnt offering pictures the sacrifice of Jesus, and the times correspond to the hours he endured on the cross. The fact that the peace offerings were to be laid on top of the continual burnt offering shows the peace with God we can attain is based first on the sacrifice of Christ.

It also shows that peace with God is attained when individuals freely offer themselves in consecration and delight in doing God’s will, just as Jesus did. Peace offerings were offered for a number of reasons.

Leviticus 7:11,12,15-16: (KJV) 11 And this is the law of the sacrifice of peace offerings, which he shall offer unto the LORD. 12 If he offer it for a thanksgiving, then he shall offer with the sacrifice of thanksgiving unleavened cakes mingled with oil, and unleavened wafers anointed with oil, and cakes mingled with oil, of fine flour, fried. 15 And the flesh of the sacrifice of his peace offerings for thanksgiving shall be eaten the same day that it is offered; he shall not leave any of it until the morning. 16 But if the sacrifice of his offering be a vow, or a voluntary offering, it shall be eaten the same day that he offereth his sacrifice: and on the morrow also the remainder of it shall be eaten:

The burnt offering is that basic gratitude—it is remarkable that the lamb in the morning at nine o’clock and the lamb in the evening at three o’clock really does show Jesus’ sacrifice. But this peace offering laid on top of the burnt offering is the DEVOTION that comes from the GRATITUDE which is all because JUSTICE was satisfied.

The cakes and wafers with oil were fried and offered with the lambs of the peace offering.

To simplify the concept of burnt offerings, just think of willingness. Leviticus 1 says an individual brought a burnt offering of his own voluntary will. God does not force anyone to come to Him or to appreciate what He is doing. Today, in our Bible studies, we must do the work and try to understand His plan. If we understand it, the correct response is to want to offer something in return.

Leviticus 1 is the Law for the people’s burnt offerings. Thus, it relates more to the next age, when mankind will be brought back in the resurrection. In that chapter, Israelites were allowed to offer various animals, from a young bull to even a pigeon. It was up to each individual what offering level they wanted to bring.

Because this illustrates the work of the coming kingdom when the world will begin to learn about God, different levels of appreciation and willingness are pictured. At first, some may only be able to express gratitude at the pigeon level. But as the age progresses and people see the magnitude of what God is doing, deeper expressions will come, pictured by the offering of a bull.

Then, we get to the peace offering, where we see the fundamental goal of God’s plan. We get a wonderful insight showing that peace offerings represent consecration to God when we look at Leviticus 8. It’s the chapter that describes the consecration of Israel’s priesthood.

We want to focus on the peace offering in Leviticus 8 because this chapter helps define its meaning. The peace offering was called the ram of consecration. After Moses killed it, he took the ram's blood and put it on the priests’ right ear, right thumb and right big toe.

By doing this, he was saying this priesthood is now consecrated to serve God as priests and be the go-between between God and the nation of Israel. The fact that it is their ear, their right thumb and their toe indicates their powers and their strength are all devoted to God.

That marvelous symbolism represents a Christian’s consecration to God and the work we are being called to do with mankind. We are being prepared to counsel and guide the world back to God, which requires our dedication in this life.

The point is that this ram of consecration was a peace offering—not a sin offering or even a burnt offering. Although this particular peace offering was unique in how the blood was placed on the priest, it helps us define the general symbolism of peace offerings. They represent an individual’s consecration to God—whether it be in this life as a prospective member of the royal priesthood or in the kingdom, as mankind offers themselves to God in consecration.

Peace offerings then show us that the ultimate goal of God’s plan is the full consecration of every heart, whether in this present age among faithful Christians or the age to come when the knowledge of the Lord fills the earth.

When we read the details of the blood on the ear, right thumb and right big toe, we realize the depth of what those rituals described. God is sending a message through these meticulously-placed sacrifices, showing us His plan unfolding with Jesus at the center of it all. There are also those who are following him right by his side.

We see these things being shouted out in the Old Testament sacrifices in the Tabernacle. How can this affect us and change the way we live our lives in Christ?

This text shows us Jesus’ powerful example of what we are to follow:

Psalm 40:6-8: (KJV) 6 Sacrifice and offering Thou didst not desire; mine ears hast Thou opened: burnt offering and sin offering hast Thou not required. 7 Then said I, Lo, I come: in the volume of the book it is written of me, 8 I delight to do Thy will, O my God: yea, Thy law is within my heart.

Think about what verse 6 is saying. We are dwelling on the sin offering, and the burnt offering, yet this scripture says that burnt offering and sin offering hast Thou not required. What was required of Jesus? God required Jesus to be the offering, not just for justice, but for the joy of serving the Lord! I delight to do Thy will, O my God. THAT is the power of all of this. THAT proves that those pictures were fulfilled entirely and clearly in Jesus. We are kind of breathless because these scriptures have so much power!

The main point of the Tabernacle is better seen when we relate these three categories of offerings to one another.

Sin offerings: These provide a covering of sins. But, the Day of Atonement shows us that atonement has two parts:

The first part is the covering of sins by the blood of Christ.

The second part is the work of removing sin from every heart. For mankind, that’s the work of God’s kingdom.

Burnt offerings: Here we see God is looking for our free-will expressions of gratitude, whether now in this age or the age to come for the world. He does not force anyone to serve Him or express thanks. But there are great blessings for those who do.

Peace offerings: Finally, the goal of God’s plan is to establish our peace with God through personal consecration, represented in the peace offerings.

To get the whole picture, we must look at how these offerings are interrelated.

My prayer for everyone is that at some point, either in this life or in the future, each of us can become a sweet savor to God! It was a blessing to be with you.

There is power in putting the Old Testament into the context of the full meaning of God's plan. The Tabernacle sacrifices were not just there to keep the Israelites busy. They were there to paint the amazing picture of what Jesus will bring to the reconciliation of mankind, what his true followers will add and how the whole world will be able to receive this. Then, as a result of the sin offering, the world will be able to feel that gratitude and make those figurative burnt offerings and peace offerings in their lives in the kingdom. They can say God is our God FOREVER, and we are eternally grateful and devoted to Him!

That is what those sacrifices are all about!

So, what’s the difference between burnt offerings and sin offerings? For Rick, Jonathan, Julie and Christian Questions… …Think about it!

All scriptures cited are from the NASB1995 translation unless otherwise noted.

To help illustrate our question, What can we as individuals possibly offer to help bring the world back to a relationship with God?

Crayon Masterpieces By Loni Boyd

Sometimes, when an opportunity to serve the Lord or the brethren comes my way, I become exuberant, excited, passionate and ready to take action…but then, Satan seems to creep in and plants the thought, "I don’t know if I would be very good at that. Maybe I should step aside and let others do that work. I am sure there are others who would be better than me."

I started to contemplate this.

Does it matter how good we are at something that we do for the Lord? I thought about when I was a child, and I loved to color and draw pictures. For as long as I can remember into my childhood, I was always working on some little art project of my own or an art project from school. I would complete my little works of art and proudly present them to my parents.

I think we all can relate to this in one way or another. We have been that child or we have received artwork from a child or witnessed that same scene take place after a Sunday school session. We've seen a child present their work to their parent or grandparent and eagerly search their face for a smile of approval. In most cases, the art of a child is not a grand artwork that would be exhibited in a museum….not even close. But somehow, the reaction from a loving parent when they see their child has given a sincere effort to create something and offers it to them as a gift, always seems to be positive.

How can this be, and what does this mean for us?

A parent does not look at the merit of the work their child presents as an art critic would analyze a work of art, a symphony or an opera and then give a critique on execution and artist merit. Rather, they see the effort and love that went in, and that is what makes it beautiful, acceptable and worthy of being displayed on the "refrigerator art gallery."

But what if every child paused before putting crayon to paper and thought, "I don't think this will be very good. Maybe I had better let the other kids do the coloring," There would be a world filled with a whole lot of empty refrigerators and children too afraid to express love to their parents because it might not be good enough!

We are all just a bunch of kids with a box of crayons trying to put some masterpiece together to present to our Lord. None of it will ever be much more than some colorful scribblings, but to our Almighty loving parent, the God of the Universe… who paints sunsets across the sky and sculpts mountain ranges upon the earth and decorates the night with a million bright stars….a child's offering of love is a masterpiece.

So whatever you do for the Lord, do it with your might and your love, and present it as that eager child presents a scribbling of colors. And know that He has perfect parent eyes, and He smiles….And, if He had a refrigerator, your work would have its place!
