Parents and Bible class teachers, continue your studies on this topic with these short, animated videos for kids at ChristianQuestions.com/YouTube:

- Why do we die? https://christianquestions.com/cq-videos/videos-cq-kids/cqkids-why-die/
- Why didn’t God stop Satan from tempting Eve? https://christianquestions.com/cq-videos/videos-cq-kids/satan-tempting-eve/
- What is sin and what can we do about it? https://christianquestions.com/cq-videos/videos-cq-kids/what-is-sin-what-can-we-do/

The Bible does not indicate the type of fruit eaten from the Tree of Knowledge of Good and Evil. Traditionally, it is shown as an apple. Perhaps it is, because the Latin translation of the tree name— lignumque scientiae boni et mali—where the Latin word for evil malum (which appears as mali in the Latin Vulgate Translation) sounds almost the same as the Latin word for an apple—mālum.

### Study Questions

https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/1362-tree-of-life/

1. What was the Tree of Life to Adam and Eve? What was the purpose of the Tree of the Knowledge of Good

and Evil? (Genesis 2:9) How do the two trees fit into God’s plan?

2. Genesis 1 tells us God created man “in His own image.” What might this include? (Genesis 1:27-28)

3. Where were the two named trees planted? Locating the trees close to each other inevitably would draw

what kind of lessons for Adam and Eve? Why did God have Adam alone in the garden for a time? (Genesis 2:8-9,16-17,23-25)

4. Describe Satan’s deception of Eve. Why did it work? How does this relate to 1 John 2:16? Draw a

comparison of the reasons behind Eve’s deception and 1 John 2:16. (Genesis 3:1-7)

Think of an experience with sin that made you want to hide from God. Now think of one that made you want to go to Him. What about these experiences caused these reactions?

5. State the consequence, then elaborate:

a. Satan’s consequence: (Genesis 3:15, Isaiah 114:14)

b. Eve’s consequence: (Genesis 3:16)

c. Adam’s consequence: (Genesis 3:17-19)

d. Adam and Eve’s final consequence: (Genesis 3:22-23)

e. How does disobedience affect God’s gift of eternal life?

6. How is Jesus the life-giving power of the Tree of Life to all of resurrected mankind? What is the role of

the Tree of Life picture in the future kingdom of God? Though all will be resurrected, what will be expected of them in order to gain eternal life? (Romans 5:18, Revelation 22:1-3,14, Acts 24:15)

7. How are Jesus’ faithful followers in heaven “trees of righteousness” to mankind in the kingdom?

What will their role be?

8. Have the lessons from the Tree of Knowledge settled into your heart? How are your choices affecting

your righteousness?

## Episode 1363 — How Do You Know If God Is Calling You?
Published: 2024-12-09
Episode page: https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/god-calling/
Rewind PDF: https://resources.christianquestions.com/wp-content/uploads/2024/12/13140511/CQ_Rewind_12-09-24_How_Do_You_Know_If_God_Is_Calling_You.pdf

John 6:44: No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him; and I will raise him up on the last day.

How does someone become a follower of Christ? Does it begin by becoming curious about this Jesus person and all of the world-changing influence that he has? Perhaps we are living in fear and are searching for a kind of security that is different and higher than anything else we know. Maybe we’re overwhelmed with despair in our lives, and we’re searching for powerful hope and encouragement. Or perhaps we have been awakened to the realization that there is more to life than what this world has to offer, much more. While any of these things can play a role in coming to Christ, the one common factor in all cases of true discipleship is beyond us. It is God drawing us to Him through Jesus. So, how does that work? How can we identify the drawing of God and differentiate it from our own emotions?

Can we make a checklist of the top 10 things to look for? Unfortunately not. Because each of us has unique lives with unique circumstances, God calls people in many different ways. Instead of a checklist, we will “check into” the principles God uses to call us. We will define and focus on four words:

We’ll discuss these four basic motivations for seeking God (there may be others), as they can be tools for discerning where God may be leading.

First, let’s establish that it is God who draws individuals to follow Christ:

John 6:44: No one can come to me unless the Father who sent me draws him; and I will raise him up on the last day.

This is a straightforward statement by Jesus: God must draw us; then we can come to Jesus.

Jesus taught God’s selective drawing to discipleship in Christ several times.

Here is one example:

Matthew 11:25–26: 25 At that time Jesus said, I praise You, Father, Lord of heaven and earth, that You have hidden these things from the wise and intelligent and have revealed them to infants. 26 Yes, Father, for this way was well-pleasing in Your sight.

It’s a LIMITED call!

But it seems unfair that the call to a heavenly reward is by invitation only. What about everyone else?

It is a limited call, but NOT limited salvation. There are two parts to salvation, as shown by many scriptures. It’s imperative to remember that salvation always comes to the human race through Jesus.

1. First, salvation comes by invitation—that is, the salvation that leads to discipleship and the heavenly call.

2. Second, salvation comes to the rest of humanity through Jesus by a merciful, earthly resurrection.

Not everyone is called to the heavenly calling at this time. But everyone has the opportunity for salvation—just at different times.

See Bonus Material for more on this point and recommended episodes where we have discussed this in great detail.

Jesus appeals to the burdened of the world to find strength and hope in him:

Matthew 11:28-30: 28 Come to me, all who are weary and heavy-laden, and I will give you rest. 29 Take my yoke upon you and learn from me, for I am gentle and humble in heart, and YOU WILL FIND REST FOR YOUR SOULS. 30 For my yoke is easy and my burden is light.

Don’t we all feel weary and heavy-laden at times? It’s an UPLIFTING call!

Putting his yoke upon us as we walk with him brings peace.

While this is inspiring, we also need to have in mind the other part of God’s call to Christ’s followers:

Luke 9:23–24: 23 And he was saying to them all, If anyone wishes to come after me, he must deny himself, and take up his cross daily and follow me. 24 For whoever wishes to save his life will lose it, but whoever loses his life for my sake, he is the one who will save it.

Uplifting, yes, but it’s a DIFFICULT call! We still have to do our part.

Discipleship is not something to take lightly. We need endurance to get through its challenges. This is a lifelong commitment. The difficult part is to take up his cross daily. In Jesus’ day, that meant torturous death. Jesus used those words plainly and clearly. We see the contrast between being uplifted and inspired during trials, yet taking up our (figurative) crosses (going through those trials, experiences and challenges). Being drawn to Jesus through God is a big deal, but how does this happen?

ARE WE BEING CALLED TO EARTHLY HAPPINESS?

Israel was promised this when they were given God’s Law:

Deuteronomy 28:1-3: 1 Now it shall be, if you diligently obey the LORD your God, being careful to do all His commandments which I command you today, the LORD your God will set you high above all the nations of the earth. 2 All these blessings will come upon you and overtake you if you obey the LORD your God: 3 Blessed shall you be in the city, and blessed shall you be in the country.

We remember this was God’s covenant with Israel as a nation—they would have physical, earthly prosperity as a reward for being faithful to God. Conversely, they would have physical adversity as a punishment for disobedience and neglect of their religious obligations.

Is the Christian promised these same physical blessings?

In Deuteronomy, Israel was promised that if they follow after God, they would have earthly happiness. This is the opposite of Jesus’ instruction in Luke saying the Christian must take up his cross daily and follow me. We are not being called to a life of ease; rather, to a discipleship requiring self-sacrifice.

We will highlight four scriptural examples of individuals being called from God to Christ.

We seek happiness, especially when we are in some form of DESPAIR or lack of hope. CORNELIUS had a measure of despair as he was a Roman Centurion who, by definition, was out of God’s favor.

Let’s see how that turned around and what it means in terms of God calling us:

Acts 10:2-5: 2 Cornelius was a devout man and one who feared (revered) God with all his household, and gave many alms to the Jewish people and prayed to God continually.

What a nice description:

He was devout, respecting Judaism and its Law.

He and his household revered the true God and not the pagan gods of Rome.

He gave alms—he was generous to the people of Israel.

He prayed to the God of Israel.

Not having outward proof of God’s presence and blessing in his life didn’t stop him from his worship and praise. He was definitely different from those around him!

3 About the ninth hour of the day he clearly saw in a vision an angel of God who had just come in and said to him, Cornelius! 4 …Your prayers and alms have ascended as a memorial before God. 5 Now…send for a man named…Peter.

Cornelius was told to send his men to Joppa to find Peter and invite him to his home.

Cornelius had been living a life that honored the God of Israel even though, as a Roman, he had no right to Israel’s favor. God would dramatically bless him, but only at the appropriate time.

We labeled him with “DESPAIR” for being out of God’s favor. In another sense, he obviously couldn’t find what he was looking for in either paganism or his military career. Many people draw closer to God when what the world has to offer doesn’t satisfy them.

Desperation often brings loneliness. We can imagine this is what Cornelius felt, being so different from those around him. But he had this wonderful, great faith. God told the Apostle Peter in a vision to go and see Cornelius, who would become the first Gentile convert.

It would be a dramatic change at this time for God to directly deal with anyone who was not Jewish. Cornelius, who as a Roman soldier was so far away from God’s favor, would be first in line to receive it. God’s grace would answer his desperation.

Peter arrives at his home:

Acts 10:27–29: 27 As he (Peter) talked with him, he entered and found many people assembled. 28 And he said to them, You yourselves know how unlawful it is for a man who is a Jew to associate with a foreigner or to visit him; and yet God has shown me that I should not call any man unholy or unclean. 29 That is why I came without even raising any objection when I was sent for. So I ask, for what reason did you send for me?

Peter walks into Cornelius’ house and basically says, I’m breaking the Jewish Law, but I am here because God has shown me that I should not call any man unclean. What an incredible introduction!

Cornelius then speaks of his vision and the instructions he was given to send his servants for Peter. God’s call to Cornelius and his household would now be proven by the evidence of God’s spirit falling upon them and what happens next.

We see the desperate loneliness, the separation from God, is about to be bridged because God is drawing Cornelius:

Acts 10:45–48: 45 All the circumcised believers who came with Peter were amazed, because the gift of the holy spirit had been poured out on the Gentiles also. 46 For they were hearing them speaking with tongues and exalting God. Then Peter answered, 47 Surely no one can refuse the water for these to be baptized who have received the holy spirit just as we did, can he? 48 And he ordered them to be baptized in the name of Jesus Christ…

Peter and Cornelius were both chosen for this monumental mission of God’s favor going to the Gentiles for the first time because they had the humility to accept God. Cornelius and his household were living outside of God’s favor and yet were reverencing Him. God called those willing hearts to discipleship once the Jews rejected Jesus and the call was opened to the Gentiles.

I see an interesting parallel between Cornelius and myself. In my personal experience, “despair” was the theme for me 40 years ago. I literally hit bottom in my life, feeling lonely and isolated. There was nowhere else to look but up. I begged God to give me direction in my life, not just in a moment but over a period of time. He overruled that an individual came into my life to point the way. It did take some time for me to understand God's plan of salvation for all before I answered God's call. But since I answered it, I have been truly blessed.

This is a common experience. God often calls us through DESPAIR. We search for earthly happiness but find only DESPAIR and loneliness.

Rather than being lost in DESPAIR, God gave Cornelius and Jonathan a destination- driven life. Not desperation-driven, but a destination-driven life. Where was it driven to? A spiritually happy, spiritually contented, spiritually fulfilled life.

This is a tool God can use when we’re searching. God knows if He is going to draw us. He can use our DESPAIR and loneliness to bring us closer to Him, to bring us up higher.

We should be searching for spiritual happiness, not physical happiness.

To determine if God is calling us, ask:

Where am I?

What is happening in my life?

How is God directing these experiences?

This can be both daunting and very encouraging. God’s call will come in His time and in His way, and it’s always for our best benefit!

God’s call took Cornelius from being on the outside looking in to being a disciple. How does it work with someone who was already favored?

Just because Israel had been God’s chosen people didn’t mean that everything would come easily to them. On the contrary, that favor provided unique challenges. Our next example of God’s calling focuses on one who had been dramatically favored and yet still needed to be dramatically awakened.

ARE WE BEING CALLED TO EARTHLY ABUNDANCE?

Israel was promised this when they were given God’s Law:

Deuteronomy 28:4–6: 4 Blessed shall be the offspring of your body and the produce of your ground and the offspring of your beasts, the increase of your herd and the young of your flock. 5 Blessed shall be your basket and your kneading bowl. 6 Blessed shall you be when you come in, and blessed shall you be when you go out.

You may recognize the phrase from the King James Version—Blessed shall be thy basket and thy store. The Hebrew word has the sense of the swelling of rising dough. Dough rises as it rests after kneading; it gets bigger. An obedient nation of Israel would be blessed in every way. Is this what Christians should expect? The so-called Prosperity Gospel uses Old Testament texts like these to answer “yes.”

As was mentioned earlier, Jesus did not focus on gaining wealth. He focused on a different kind of abundance that had nothing to do with earthly things and circumstances. Those who look for earthly rewards from God need to realize that this was NEVER the focus of God’s call to Christianity.

One example is Jesus speaking to the rich young ruler:

Mark 10:20–22: 20 And he said to him, Teacher, I have kept all these things from my youth up. 21 Looking at him, Jesus felt a love for him and said to him, One thing you lack: go and sell all you possess and give to the poor, and you will have treasure in heaven; and come, follow me. 22 But at these words he was saddened, and he went away grieving, for he was one who owned much property.

In other words, it’s not about what you have but who you become. Jesus was asking, Are you willing to be selfless and follow me? He continually showed that earthly abundance was never on his mind nor in his teaching. If we try to put it there, we are misrepresenting our Lord’s words.

Let’s look at one more example:

Matthew 8:19–20: 19 Then a scribe came and said to him, Teacher, I will follow you wherever you go. 20 Jesus said to him, The foxes have holes and the birds of the air have nests, but the Son of Man has nowhere to lay his head.

It’s a SELF-SACRIFICING call!

Rather than earthly abundance, sometimes following Jesus can result in the lack of earthly things. The scribe says, Teacher, I will follow you wherever you go. Jesus answers, You don’t understand, I don’t know where I’m sleeping tomorrow! You are in a position of respect, but I am not about earthly power, authority, respect, honor and comfort.

Continuing with our four scriptural examples of individuals being called from God to Christ:

The Apostle Paul is a wonderful example of a great AWAKENING and needing to be jolted out of a life that was sincerely but improperly focused on abundance and power. Paul was a powerful individual in society—a Pharisee—and he was driven to eradicate Christianity.

God had different plans for Saul, and Jesus appeared to him in a vision:

Acts 9:1–6: 1 Now Saul, still breathing threats and murder against the disciples of the Lord, (was on his way to Damascus) 2 …so that if he found any belonging to the Way, (Christians) both men and women (he was destroying families!), he might bring them bound to Jerusalem. 3 As he was traveling… suddenly a light from heaven flashed around him; 4 and he fell to the ground and heard a voice saying to him, Saul, Saul, why are you persecuting me? 5 And he said, Who are you, Lord? And he said, I am Jesus whom you are persecuting, 6 but get up and enter the city, and it will be told you what you must do.

Saul loved God, but for him, the end justified the means. As a man of power and clout, he did not hesitate to break the Jewish Law in horrific ways. He knew better. He was smart. He understood the principles behind godliness but let his earthly thinking, desire and focus get in the way. He needed a dramatic AWAKENING, and it happened in an instant.

Sometimes, our own AWAKENING is dramatic, and sometimes it’s a process.

With the four descriptions we are looking at, I identify mostly with “awakening.” I grew up in a religious household. My grandfather and great-grandfathers were ministers, and it was simply expected that I would be in church every Sunday. I’m grateful I grew up in an environment where we were allowed to ask questions and make the Scriptures our own. While I believed, I wasn’t ready for a lifelong commitment to God until, as I’ve spoken about before on this podcast in more detail, 9/11 happened here in America. I wanted to be able to ultimately help people on a grand scale in the kingdom and realized how fragile our existence is here. However I could serve God now and in the future would be up to Him.

God drew Julie from a spiritually healthy environment to be AWAKENED and engaged on a higher level. God drew Jonathan out of DESPAIR and loneliness to something so much

bigger, higher and stronger. It is important for us to recognize that God works differently in drawing different individuals depending on their circumstances.

Let’s get back to our acount of Saul of Tarsus. Jesus’ disciple, Ananias, was instructed in a vision to seek out Saul. But he had doubts, as Saul was well-known for his attempts to crush Christianity.

Saul could have put Ananias in prison or even taken his life. Ananias had every right to be bitter towards Saul, an enemy of Christians. His friend Stephen was stoned to death at Saul’s word.

Ananias needed encouragement, and Jesus emphatically answered his doubts:

Acts 9:15–19: 15 But the Lord said to him, Go, for he is a chosen instrument of mine, to bear my name before the Gentiles and kings and the sons of Israel; 16 for I will show him how much he must suffer for my name’s sake.

While Jesus was speaking to Ananias in a vision, Saul of Tarsus was praying repeatedly. He could not see because he was blind from the bright light when Jesus appeared to him in a vision, but he prayed because the vision caused him to realize his life’s purpose must change.

17 So Ananias departed and entered the house, and after laying his hands on him said, Brother Saul, the Lord Jesus, who appeared to you on the road by which you were coming, has sent me so that you may regain your sight and be filled with the holy spirit. 18 And immediately there fell from his eyes something like scales, and he regained his sight, and he got up and was baptized; 19 and he took food and was strengthened…

Paul gave up his former life of prestige; he received no physical abundance as a Christian. In fact, he was beaten up, imprisoned and shipwrecked; people tried to kill him—nothing like his former life. Who in the New Testament, after becoming a Christian, became filthy rich? Or super powerful? There isn’t anyone because the call to Christianity from God through Christ is entirely different.

This awakening rebuilt Saul’s approach to serving God. He would now be a chosen vessel of Jesus:

Acts 9:19–22: 19 …Now for several days he was with the disciples who were at Damascus, 20 and immediately he began to proclaim Jesus in the synagogues, saying, He is the son of God. 21 All those hearing him continued to be amazed, and were saying, Is this not he who in Jerusalem destroyed those who called on this name, and who had come here for the purpose of bringing them bound before the chief priests? 22 But Saul kept increasing in strength and confounding the Jews who lived at Damascus by proving that this Jesus is the Christ.

In the Bible, certain people received visions from God instructing them in what they were to do. But the New Testament Scriptures do not teach us to expect people will see visions, hear voices or experience miracles to draw them to Jesus. These scriptural accounts were very rare and for a specific time and purpose. Today, the pull toward God is the response of an honest heart to God’s wonderful, righteous love (Luke 8:15).

However, everything we need to know comes through the word of God. We do not audibly hear the voice, but we have the word of God in the Bible. It also contains the words of Jesus, giving us guidance as to how we should respond.

The Apostle Paul needed an AWAKENING. He had earthly prestige and prosperity, which he used to persecute those he deemed enemies of God.

He was not only awakened, but he was made alive to true spiritual growth and spiritual abundance. These had nothing to do with what he owned or the prestige he once had. It would now be all about the abundance of servitude under Christ.

What are we willing to walk away from, and what are we willing to walk towards? Are we looking to be AWAKENED so we can become alive on a spiritual level? This is what the call of God through Christ is all about.

Waking up and hearing God’s call means we need to change direction. This means God is ready for us, but are we ready for Him?

So far, we have seen despair become spiritual happiness and an awakening become spiritual abundance. What’s next?

Identifying our personal mental and heart conditions in which God may be calling us is an important exercise. Just as important is weighing in on how we respond to these things. It’s one thing to feel a certain way and to want to pursue knowing God, and another thing entirely to act on that desire when God opens the door.

ARE WE BEING CALLED TO EARTHLY PROTECTION?

Israel was promised this when they were given God’s Law:

Deuteronomy 28:7: The LORD shall cause your enemies who rise up against you to be defeated before you; they will come out against you one way and will flee before you seven ways.

Israel would be blessed in their warfare. Is this what we can expect? Will all of our enemies that come at us run away in terror?

No. The call of God through Christ is to give up our own wills in daily self-sacrifice, and sometimes we will experience dangerous situations. This is a call to possibly lose prestige, power and authority. Ananias had to approach the known Christian persecutor, Paul. Paul gave up his high position of being a Pharisee. The call is not about having a guarantee of earthly protection.

Jesus emphatically taught us that discipleship is a difficult and dangerous journey:

John 15:18,20: 18 If the world hates you, you know that it has hated me before it hated you. 20 Remember the word that I said to you, A slave is not greater than his master. If they persecuted me, they will also persecute you; if they kept my word, they will keep yours also.

This doesn’t sound like earthly protection!

It’s a CHALLENGING call!

If we are wondering if God is calling us, we should look for God’s providence in our experiences that require us to change or bear up under difficulties. Jesus stated it plainly: If they persecuted me, they will also persecute you.

Jesus also taught us there needs to be a strong consistency as we follow him as disciples:

Matthew 5:13: You are the salt of the earth; but if the salt has become tasteless, how can it be made salty again?...

Salt enhances flavor, so it is a desired commodity. It is also a food preservative (especially important in ancient times), so it is a required commodity.

It also was a necessary commodity for some Old Testament sacrifices:

Leviticus 2:13: Every grain offering of yours, moreover, you shall season with salt…

Salt is woven in and out of scriptural understanding. It pictures the strength, focus and fidelity of our character in Christ. We are to be a heatlhful, preserving element in the midst of a world of moral decay. If we are in him, our strength of character depends on him. We must learn to be in line with Christ's pattern so the holy spirit (God’s power and influence) can accomplish its work in us.

It’s a CHARACTER-BUILDING call!

How does this differ from having God’s providence protecting us? If we don’t have the earthly protection given to the Old Testament Jewish people, how does God’s providence protect us now as Christians?

God’s providence doesn’t necessarily protect us from earthly foes. He protects us so that our spiritual growth and maturity can be accomplished through His will and in His way. Stephen and Paul were marched off to death—though “protected” from the standpoint of their spiritual well-being even as they were killed. They were protected because this was God’s will, His providence unfolding. God’s protection of those who are called is not about keeping everything in our earthly lives in order and having our enemies running away in seven different directions. It is the protection that allows us to follow the will of God in His way. God will protect us in whatever way is for our highest spiritual welfare.

Our next example is of Jesus going to Jericho and meeting the wealthy chief tax collector named Zaccheus. Tax collectors, also known as “publicans,” were Jewish public officials authorized by Rome to collect Roman taxes from the Jews. Not only were they despised by the people as traitors, but they had the bad reputation of overcharging and extortion.

Luke 19:1–4: 1 He (Jesus) entered Jericho and was passing through. 2 And there was a man called by the name of Zaccheus; he was a chief tax collector and he was rich. 3 Zaccheus was trying to see who Jesus was, and was unable because of the crowd, for he was small in stature. 4 So he ran on ahead and climbed up into a sycamore tree in order to see him, for he was about to pass through that way.

Zaccheus could hear Jesus, but he couldn’t see him. His CURIOSITY had now become a driving force. He had heard about Jesus and was listening to him, but he was short and could not physically see him. Zaccheus climbed a sycamore tree for the sole purpose of seeing the man speaking. His CURIOSITY to see this man uttering such truths was stronger than anything he had ever felt and would become the greatest blessing of his entire life.

Luke 19:5–7: 5 When Jesus came to the place, he looked up and said to him, Zaccheus, hurry and come down, for today I must stay at your house.

Imagine among all the throngs of people, he not only noticed Zaccheus but called him by name and announced that he, Jesus, would be an honored guest at his home.

6 And he hurried and came down and received him gladly. 7 When they saw it, they all began to grumble, saying, He has gone to be the guest of a man who is a sinner.

The crowd despised Zaccheus as a tax collector, yet Jesus responded in an unexpectedly positive way. Why? Jesus made it abundantly clear that anyone’s status within their culture was of no consequence when it came to responding to the message.

Jesus was looking for the right heart attitude and was standing before a man with a full heart:

Luke 19:8: Zaccheus stopped and said to the Lord, Behold, Lord, half of my possessions I will give to the poor, and if I have defrauded anyone of anything, I will give back four times as much.

Zaccheus knew that under the Jewish Law, restitution was required for taking advantage of another. We read in Numbers 5:6-7 that the guilty man had to repay what he defrauded plus an extra one-fifth or 20 percent. Zaccheus went well beyond this requirement by giving half of his wealth to the poor plus four times what he cheated people out of. This was a dramatic repentance and an overwhelming response! If he had offered 30 or 40 percent instead of the required 20, we would have thought that was generous. But no! He gave four times as much—400 percent—because his heart was so moved. Jesus saw that in him. Zaccheus’ CURIOSITY put him in a position so God could bless him through Jesus.

Jesus saw the heart of this curious outcast and proclaimed salvation and divine protection for him:

Luke 19:9: And Jesus said to him, Today salvation has come to this house, because he, too, is a son of Abraham. 10 For the Son of Man has come to seek and to save that which was lost.

There we see divine protection. His CURIOSITY wasn’t about finding earthly protection, even though someone as despised in his business was likely looking over his shoulder a lot. Instead of being just a curious individual looking for earthly protection, Jesus gave him clarity in his life. Clarity for what? The clarity of seeing that he was spiritually protected and spiritually lifted up, and his life was spiritually changed so he could abide in God through Christ.

God’s call may come when we are in a position of looking and wondering. Suddenly, something opens up, and we see something bigger and stronger than we saw before. It is powerful and all about answering God’s call to come up higher.

We’ve observed that some of our Christian Questions listeners have metaphorically “climbed up the tree” to hear more—beyond what they were hearing in their churches or in the traditions with which they grew up.

We invite our listeners to ask those tough questions and dig deeper into the Bible with us each week.

Write us at inspiration@ChristianQuestions.com.

Our point is to be looking! Have that curiosity! If it is drawn in the right direction and God sees in us someone He can use as part of the body of Christ, He will call us. The question is, will we be willing, ready and able to answer and grow into whatever He is calling us towards?

This is yet another example of how God’s call brings us out of our own perceptions and into His way of seeing and living our lives!

We have seen examples of God using despair, awakening and curiosity to bring us to Him. How might God use fear?

FEAR can be a dramatic motivator and a dramatic tool that shuts us down. God certainly can use our fears if we remain open to the change that His presence in our lives demands. As we will see, the Apostle Peter is a good example of how God through Jesus can turn FEAR into positive action.

ARE WE BEING CALLED TO EARTHLY PEACE?

Israel was promised this when they were given God’s Law:

Deuteronomy 28:9–10: 9 The LORD will establish you as a holy people to Himself, as He swore to you, if you keep the commandments of the LORD your God and walk in His ways. 10 So all the peoples of the earth will see that you are called by the name of the LORD, and they will be afraid of you.

The best blessing Israel received was having a special relationship with God. If they kept His commandments, Israel would be a holy people.

But how far should we spiritualize this lesson? Can we appropriate this promise that everyone will be afraid of us as God’s holy people, not attacking or provoking us?

No, we can’t. Our life will fall into place however God’s providence directs it.

The Apostle Peter clearly warns us that discipleship can bring us tumult and not peace:

1 Peter 4:12,14: 12 Beloved, do not be surprised at the fiery ordeal among you, which comes upon you for your testing, as though some strange thing were happening to you; 14 If you are reviled for the name of Christ, you are blessed, because the spirit of glory and of God rests on you.

It’s an ENDURANCE-BASED call!

Descriptions of a fiery ordeal and being reviled for the name of Christ don’t seem like the Christian is promised earthly peace!

We’re not! The call is not about this life and this world. It is about what God calls us to.

God’s call provokes an internal struggle between our humanity and our desire to serve God. There are these two forces at odds inside us:

Romans 7:21–25: 21 I find then the principle that evil is present in me, the one who wants to do good. 22 For I joyfully concur with the law of God in the inner man, 23 but I see a different law in the members of my body, waging war against the law of my mind and making me a prisoner of the law of sin which is in my members. 24 Wretched man that I am! Who will set me free from the body of this death? 25 Thanks be to God through Jesus Christ our Lord!...

It’s a call to INTENTIONAL FOCUS!

Rather than the promise of a completely peaceful existence, we have work to do—we are to overcome the world, the temptations of Satan, our own ego, our brokenness, our past and our present.

It’s wonderful to love Jesus, but that’s just the beginning. Transformation is required, and change is difficult. This change requires us to go through that Romans 7 challenge of fighting the two forces inside of us.

Being called to follow Christ means being at war with our old physical person and saying, I need to rise to a spiritually-minded individual like Jesus was. Our call is not to an earthly peace.

Let’s look at the example of the Apostle Peter. Before he was called Peter, his name was Simon. The call of Simon to follow Jesus came in stages. Simon was impetuous and bold, and yet the call to leave his entire life behind and follow Jesus was scary.

Here is how Jesus approached that fear. This was their first encounter:

John 1:41–42: 41 He (Andrew) found first his own brother Simon and said to him, We have found the Messiah (which translated means Christ). 42 He brought him to Jesus. Jesus looked at him and said, You are Simon the son of John; you shall be called Cephas (which is translated Peter).

Jesus did not formally invite Simon to follow him at this point, but he did entice him with a view of the future. Jesus’ message to Simon was, I know who you are and who you can become.

We don’t see anyone else where Jesus instantly changes their name. The impetuous and powerful Simon must have been thinking, Sir, who are you, and what are you doing? Wait a minute, what’s going on? What did he have in mind for Simon?

Let’s look at their second encounter:

Matthew 4:18–20: 18 Now as Jesus was walking by the Sea of Galilee, he saw two brothers, Simon who was called Peter, and Andrew his brother, casting a net into the sea; for they were fishermen. 19 And he said to them, Follow me, and I will make you fishers of men. 20 Immediately they left their nets and followed him.

In other words, drop your work; there’s something bigger for you to do.

Remember this point because we will come back to it: they left their nets and followed him.

Jesus' message to Simon: Follow me, and built upon the promise of a new name, I will show you a new vocation.

While Simon and company left fishing to follow Jesus, they didn’t leave it permanently. They followed him for a period of time. They saw many wonderful things and better understood who he was, what he did and how he did it. But then they returned to fishing.

Fast-forward months later after Peter and his brother had been fishing all night without catching anything. That next morning, Jesus used Peter’s boat as a place to preach. He stood in the boat a little way out so he could see and speak to all the people on the shore. When Jesus was done speaking, he instructed Peter to cast his nets out and fish again.

Peter says, Wait, Lord, we spent all night fishing and didn’t catch a thing. Why would we do that again? But he listens.

Peter’s fears would be manifested. Here is where Jesus’ love, compassion and understanding conquers those fears:

Luke 5:6–11: 6 When they had done this, they enclosed a great quantity of fish, and their nets began to break; 7 so they signaled to their partners in the other boat… And they came and filled both of the boats, so that they began to sink. 8 But when Simon Peter saw that, he fell down at Jesus’ feet, saying, Go away from me Lord, for I am a sinful man! 9 For amazement had seized him and all his companions…

Essentially, he said to Jesus, this is too big. What are you doing with me? He could see the power of God working through him and it was overwhelming.

10 …Do not fear, from now on you will be catching men.

In other words, Don’t worry. I’ve got something in store for you.

11 When they had brought their boats to land, they left everything and followed him.

Did you notice that subtle point? In their second encounter Matthew said they left their nets and followed him. Months later, Luke records they left everything and followed him.

We see the growth in these men. Simon Peter had to be drawn and reassured because he was in awe and FEAR that God was working through him.

We might look at what God puts before us, the privilege of the incredible things He gives us to do and we hesitate, thinking, I can’t do this; it’s bigger than me. God’s answer to us is always, You are right, it is, so stop relying on yourself and rely on Me. This is what Peter had to learn, and we do too.

Peter was looking for earthly peace, but he had earthly struggles and FEAR. Through the call of God, Jesus gave him a focused life. It was focused on not just earthly peace and making a living, but the peace of God through Jesus during what would be very difficult trials.

The journey of the Apostle Peter went from FEAR to being focused. God can call us out of fear to something different, something higher.

We might be afraid, but we don’t have to be. We can put our fears behind us so there is room to accept what God is bringing us.

We've looked at the DESPAIR of Cornelius that was turned into being a destination- driven individual.

We have talked about the Apostle Paul's AWAKENING, which turned into his being alive to spiritual growth and spiritual abundance.

We saw Zacchaeus' CURIOSITY, which was turned into clarity of thinking. He just wanted to see—and then, not only was he able to see, but he was able to partake of and be a part of those around him through his generosity toward those he wronged.

Then, we saw the FEAR of the Apostle Peter, which turned into a focus on following and living in the peace of God.

God calls individuals in many different ways. If we believe we are being called, it is our responsibility to listen and be willing to change—to rise up and be different and follow in Jesus' footsteps.

So, how do you know if God is calling you?

For Rick, Jonathan, Julie and Christian Questions… …Think about it!

All scriptures cited are from the NASB1995 translation unless otherwise noted.

The profound results of God creating something never seen before https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/1324-new-creation/

Examining what discipleship is and what it is not https://christianquestions.com/doctrine/1170-following-jesus/

Parents and Bible class teachers, continue your studies on this topic with these short, animated videos for kids at ChristianQuestions.com/YouTube:

- What happened on the road to Damascus? https://christianquestions.com/cq-videos/videos-cq-kids/road-to-damascus/
- How do Christians receive the holy spirit? https://christianquestions.com/cq-videos/videos-cq-kids/how-to-receive-the-holy-spirit/
